mercantile marine—she is exceeded in size only by the

a British destroyer, was set afire

her crew.

By RICHARD CAPELL 


“Dally Telegraph” War Correspon- 
dent with the French Army 

A commonplace of every dny In 
the saying that this war begins 
where the other left olf. ; 

You hear it said again and ogain 
when the mighty engines of destruc- 
tion and defence: are exhibited: for 
your admiration and nwe. But it fs 
also ‘true of the treatment nccorded 
to the humble human servants of the 
great machine. , 

A parodox of 1D14-18 was that os 
time went on and the people at 

‘ home felt the pinch more and more 
the food we got at the front grew 
more tolerable. 

Early in the war we were.fed with 

“a. truly) wonderful incumpetence, 

wastcfully and sickeningly. As the 
years rolled on there came mili for 
our tea, vegetables for the stew, and 
a margarine ration. An effort was in 
fact made to provide a more or less 
balanced dict. ” 

I have asked a Jarge number of 
French soldiers in a variety af clr- 
cumstances nbout their food. Not 
one has complained, though 1 have 
had every opportunity of talking 
with them confidentially. 


» Put On Weight Paeee 

A middle-aged — reservist, who 

sported a row of medal ribbons, sald 

to me thot the soldicrs of Joffre's 

urmies would have made eyes like 

snucers at the food thnt fs seryed to 
the troops to-day. 

And be ft recalled that fn Joffre’s 
days the tastiness of French Army 
food waa the envy of the British, 
while our wastefulness was a by- 
word with the French. 

reservist, n Uttle man, told me 
that he had put on six kilogrammes, 
or nearly a stone, during his two 
months at the front thls autumn. 

.. The French soldter {s also much 
better off than he was Inst {ime in 
the provision of the French equiva- 
lent of our Y.M.C.A. huts, The 
name of this equivalent is "Le Foyer 
du Soldat." - tate te . 

It existed in a very modest way 
in 1914. Help from the Amertcan 

'Y.M.C.A. mnde possible o Jarge de- 
velopment, and in 1928 there were 
nearly, 1,600 foyers bchind the 

So many fs not too many. <A regi-~ 

‘ ment—the equivalent of our brigade 
“comes out of the line. The men 
are muddy and weary. They have 
not keen through hoavy fighting, but 
their spell in the line has, all the 
same, been no holiday, é 

They come back to billets jn barns 
and stable lofts; which do well 
enough, but are net wonderfully 
cheerful on a wet autumn evening, 


Comforts Appreciated 


- At such a time the modest com= 
forts of the foyer are well appre- 
elated. The men pour in. There 
are hot drinks to be had, 

At the counter, chocolate, cakes, 
soup, writing paper, pencils und 
fountain pens are on sole al manu- 
favturers’ prices. Many of the men 
come in for a hand of cards. They 
Play_belote—the pontoon or brag of 
the French Army, + 

Sometimes a foyer will boast the 
luxury of billlards or a ping-pong 
table. co 
But if n anlt has just come out 
‘ of tho ne what most of the men 

think about is tho Ictter home. 

The foyer du soldut is, like the 
Y.M.C.A., undenominatlonal. It may 
be sald to go further: it takes par- 
ticular care to avoid giving the im- 
pression that dt has a didactle aim. 

The directors of the foyers know 
thelr men; It would be the‘ mistake 
of mistakes to 'rafse the suspicion 
that the‘ welcome, the lighted room 
and the free writing paper were in- 
tended to influence anyone's religious 
or Srrellgtous opinions or outloots.

tresses. The directress of ench foyer

educnted classes whose business Is 

“not qniy to seo that the provision of

alr components. .

Three Tasks Carried 
Out Simultaneously 


‘ LONDON, Dee. 19 (Reuter). 
—A special commentary issued 
to-day states that the recent 
news reveals the full extent of 
the British naval power, 

While fighting successful actions in 
the North Sea and Montevideo simul- 
tancously, the Royal Navy was also 
engaged in the gigantic’ task of con- 
voylng the frst contingent of Cana- 


Sceretary, 


“dian forees~across” the“Atlanties 


The successful performance of three 
such arduous tasks over’ this wide 
urea sheds light on the foolish Ger- 
min boast of control of the North Sea 
and Athintic. 


Communism 
Growing 


In Austria 


BUDAPEST. 

SOME anthentic facts about 
the situation in Austria have 
just been made available here. 

A growth of aggressive Com- 
munism among Vienna workers, 
partly because of the pact with 
Stalin and partly because of low 
wages and the poor quality of 
food, worries the Nazis, 

Strikes have just occurred in 
several factories, Including a locomo- 
tive works, where hundreds of work- 
ers held up thelr hands in reply. to 
the question “Who is dissatisfied?” 
put by the Gestapo (Secret Police) 
after they had occupied the bulld- 
ing 

B.B.C, broadcasts are widely Nst- 
emicd to despite the ban. The broad- 
casts are criticised because of too 
much talk of Austrla’s present plight 
and too little of Austria's future 
should the democracies win,

AUSTRALIA is industrially 
prepared ‘Lo meet war conditions, 
and both Government and pri- 
vate factories are being called 
into action to assist the Allies. 

“In 1914 Australia had to depend 
on Great Britain for munitions,” sald 
Mr. Jomes Hendry, president: of ‘the 
N.S.W. Chamber of Manufactures, 


“To-day it fs most likely that’ Aus- |- 


tralia will not only help to feed the 
Allied armies, but will export muni- 


tions, 
Mobiliscd Industry 


“Now that the call has come, the 
manufacturers of Auntrolia will res- 
pond with all the resources ut their. 
disposal, Australia's safety may well 
tepend on the apbtlity of Australian 
“industry to supply all the many and 
varied requirements of modern me- 
chantsed warfare,” . 

Australia now mates almost every- 
thing necessary for modern warfare 
from ucroplanes to motor-curs, states 
the British United Presy. For nearly 
three years the Government has been 
mobilising private industry, os well 
as such units as Government railway 
workshops to supplement Govern- 
ment munition factorles.

three-year-old son of Mr. and Mrs. 
J. G, Dennistoun Sword, of Glotten- 
ham, Robertsbridge, Sussex,

from her first husband, one of the 
Army's tallest officers, this year. 
She wos granted the custody of their 
year-old baby. 

Mrs Doubeny {sti daughter of 
Princess Nicholas Galitzine. She was 
presented at Court In 1930.

THE prefix “Special to the Telegraph” 
ta uned by the “Iongkong Telegraph" to 
indicate news which [s stric copyright 
under tho provisions of the Telecommunl- 
cations Ordinance, 1938. Such news as 
dears ‘the Indication “UP ts received in

servo all rights and forbid repubtication, 
either wholly or in part without previeus 
wrrangement, 


Britain and Free Speech 


A day seldom passes jn which 
some London newapaper cloes not 
complain of the burden of war-time 
officiatdom restricting the freedom 
of British life. A render of the 
London press, recently arrived in 
England, might come to the con- 
clusion that Britain had ceased to 
be a democracy) and was now 
governed by a dictatorial bureau- 
cracy. | 

Yet if he asked himself how he 
had come to such ‘a conclusion, he 
would surely think it significant

—"Gou be merciful to us and bless

viluable thing that this world 
affords. Here is wisdom; thfs js the 
royal law; these are the lively oracies 
of God.”

Crushing Resistance 


Helsinki, Dee. 19, 
The weather was the clearest to- 


day since the war began, «the sky 
being blue with misty clouds, - 

The Russlans are reported to have 
renewed thelr attacks on the Man- 
nerhelm Line, despite heavy losses, 
apparently believing that Finland's 
small man power cannot long with- 
stand the incessant pounding. The 
latest attack was to the west of the 
middle Isthmus, 


In the past three days the Rus- 
slanis attacked with great force, slx 
different places along the Line. Fin- 
nish military observers believe that 
the Russians plan to continue the 
series of punches, hoping to keep 
their might stendily unrested, but it 
{a Indicated that Finland has more 
reserves than It is realised, including 
200,000 women enrolicd in an unpaid 
corps doing men’s work and releasing 
the latter for fighting. ¢ : 

The Russian theory of war Is the 
use of an overwhelming mass of tanks 
and. artillery, followed _by_ infantry, 
agalnst the Finnish‘ theory of skilled” 
man power using the geographical 
ond natural obstacles to mobility to 
knock out. the machines; 

This is iustrated in the official Fin- 
nish reports that 72 Russian tanks 
have been destroyed and captured in 
three days, a 

The fighting has extended 120 miles 
from the Isthmus along Lake Ladoga, 
United Press.

EMPIRE 
UNITY 


Canadians Arrive 
In England 


i .  , London, Dec, 19, 

The Canadian Expeditionary Force 
received wt very enthustastic welcome 
when the men streamed ashore in 
thousands on Sunday. 

Great ners, guarded by a formid~ 
able squadron from the Royal Navy, 
brought the soldiers into the British 
port, while a fleet of aeroplanes 
patrolled overhead, guarding agatnst 
any. surprise attack, 

» The troops represent every part of 
Canada, and include some Red 
Indians, ; 

Many of the Canadians served In 
the last war, but the majority are 
youngsters. v 

When the troops were drawn up on 
the pier, they heard the  Gencral 
Officer Commanding read on message 
of welcome from His Majesty the 
King, who sald: ‘The British Army 
will be proud to have as comrades 
in arms the successors of those who 
came from Canada in the Great War 
and fought with a heroism that has 
never been forgotten.” 

The Canadians doffed thelr caps 
and cheered Mr, Anthony Eden, 
Secretary of State for the Dominions, 
when he officially welcomed them.’ 
Mr, Eden said: “The British people 
will never forget this’ great act of 
co-operation, The task before us 
may b¢ a long one—it certainly will 
be ‘a tough one, but in the assurance 
of the peoples of. the, Btitish Com- 
monwealth jt will result with cer- 
tainty in a final victory.’" * 

The Commander-in-Chief of: the 
Canadian Forces, Major General A. 
G. McNaughton, replied, expressing 
nratitude to the King. and to Mr. 
Eden, and to the Royal Navy for their 
splendid safeguarding of the troops 
across the Atlantic. The troops then 
entrained—for-n-camp--in-—-a-distant 
part of Britain.—Reuler, 


Bengal Assuranco 


_ _ eatcutta, Dec, 19, . 
Mr, Nalini Ranjan Sarkar, Finance 
Minister of the Bengal Government 
has resigned after abstuining from 
voting on a resolution passed by the 
Bengal Legislative Assembly assuring 
full _co-operntion in the prosecution 
of the war. 


The resolution, which wus passed 
by 82 to 42 votes, also urged the 
granting of Dominion status to India 
immediately after the war and the 
framing of a new constitution pro- 
viding for effective safeguards for the 
minorities. 

Mr. Sarkar explained his absten- 
tion,’ saying that he could not ac- 
quiesce In investing the minorities 
with power for vetoing al! political 
advancement, ° 

The Government Coalition ;Party 
subsequently passed a vote f no 
confidence in Mr, Sarkar in view of 
his total disregard of the unanimous 
Party declsion—Reuter Special,

Jack Dempsey 
Didn’t Come 


“Delayed by the heavy weather off 
the Philippines, the China Clipper 
arrived at Kal Tak Inte yesterday 
afternoon, but Jack Dempsey ex- 
heavyweight champion of the world, 
was not nboard, He hnd intended to 
visit Hongkong up to the tlme of his 
arrival in Manila, but. the Reception 
Committee which met him there 
would -not hear of It.. They had al- 
ready -planned a, full programme 
for his short stay and this looked so 
promising that Dempsey decided to 
give Hongkong 2 miss. [t is probable, 
however, that he will come after the 
Garcla—Lee fight on Saturday, 


The Clipper, which larided shortly 
before 6 p.m., brought several pas- 


sengiers, among whom was n bridal]. 


couple who will be married at noon 
to-day at the Union Church, ‘They 
are Mr, A, J. F. Barretto, young 
Manila business man, ond M 
Simone J Levy, of Manila, Mr, Bar- 
retto wos in business in Singapore 
before polt to Manila, They: will 
spend the honeymoon in Hongkong 
before returning to Manila, Several 
friends gave the couple a hearty 
erecting, 


Other passengers were Lt, John 
G. Brimmer, of the Philippines

his wife to spend the Christmas

GOLF HAS. SEEN SEVERAL novel contests, 

and quite a few were played during the last great 

_war, but the latest one has arisen out of a desire 
on the part of Joo Davis, world snooker champion, 

-to raise money for the British Red Cross. He has 
offered to meet Henry Cotton in a series of golf 


-and snooker matches, | 


All that Davis wants from 


‘and he, in turn, offers the golfer six blacks start at snooker. 
Cotton is interested, and though of the opinion that no 


handicap at snooker could be 


high enough for him; is will- 


ing to meet: Davis in both games—provided Davis gives 
him some lessons at snooker first! oO 


At the start\of the last war 
in 1914, J. N. Farrar, a native 
of Hoylake, took a 10-1 bet that 
he could not go round the Roys- 
ton Course under 100 strokes 
fully equipped in full infantry 
marching order, water bottle, 
full field kit and haversack, He 
won by going round in 94. 


Severo) golfers, including profes- 
sionals, later tried this feat, and | 


failed, ; 
* ® * 


fs in golf is the mental handicap, 
I read somewhere recently pf a 
“¢reak" match that was based on 
this factor, A low single-figure 
handicap player paired up with a 
poorer golfer, and before the match 
Offered the usual handicap of strokes 
difference, This was refused, and 
the suggestion put forward by the 
second man that he be allowed the 
privilege of saying "boo" at any 
three times during the round, and 
there would be no stroke difference. 
- This was agreed to, and the 
“psychologist” won quite comfortably 
—and with two “bog's” in hand!

were given one yard handicap per 
round he would win every cham-

quite feasible when one considers 
how many putts stop an inch or two 
from the hole, or how much differ-

Middleweight Title 


Lee Shows Considerably 


|More Punch Power Tha 
Ceferino Garcia 


Ceferino Garcia, world’s middleweight champion, may 
be rated the hardest middleweight puncher in the world 
to-day, but it was Glen Lee, the American title contender, 
who displayed the punching power in training, states the 


Manila Bulletin. 


Demonstrating the vicious mauling 


that enabled him to score victories: over the leading mid- 
dleweights in the world, including Garcia, Solly Kreiger, 
and Fred Apostoli, Lee smacked around his sparring part- 
ners with merciless abandon, and had’ three of the four 
boys who worked out with him sprawled out on the can- 


vas several times. 


He first took on Bobby Wills, 
claimant to’ the welterweight 
championship of Japan who re- 
cently returned to Manila. and 
floored him three times in the 
first round. He eased up in. the 
second round but even at that 
the Japanese champion called it

‘China Clipper Arrives 
Without Dempsey 


The China Clipper, having 
been delayed through bad 
weather off the Philippines, 
arrived at Kai Tak yesterday, 
but without Jack Dempsey, 
former heavyweight champion 
of the world. He was met 
at Manila by the formal Recep- 
tion Committee, who would 
not hear of his. continuing his 
trip and visit. Hongkong be- 
cause of the heavy schedule of 
entertainment they had ar- 
ranged for him. — 

It is probable, however, that 
he will visit the Colony follow- 
ing the Lee-Garcia fight on

HONGKONG - TELEGRAPH. 


‘Hitler Hopes For |. 


ADAME. TABOUIS,, WORLD-FAMOUS DIPLOMATIC WRITER, REVEALS TO-DAY THAT 
-HITLER, STILL IN A STATE OF INDECISION, HAS AGAIN CHANCED HIS: MIND ABOUT 


FUTURE PLANS. 


He has for the first time given his advisers to. understand that he fears to Jaunch an offensive 


in the West. 
years, if necessary. 
WILE German threats to Britain are still 
unexeeuted the Reich, in’ the last. few 
weeks, has suffered a succession of setbacks of 
all kinds—the  Anglo-French-Turkish Pact, 
Papal Eneyelieal, raising of the U.S. arms em- 
bare. speeches by Malotov and Voroshilov 
aMirminge once more‘that the Soviet. Union will 

/ not give the Reich military or material support, 
also that the Kremlin officially numbers ‘Ger- 
many among the capitalist’ States it wishes: Co 
oppose, ‘ 

The effect. on the Pulver of this series of defeats can be 
raurved by reports To have received from: neural coun 
tric sald Marlame Tobouis, 

The w plans’ for the West we have heard ro muetr 

nhout have undergone considerable changes, 

Itseems that the Fahrer, while adopting towards Hollayed 

imilar attitude to that ndepted by the Soviet ta the 
tle States, now wishes te prepare his penerals for a 
new plan. * 

This ds of much interest tous, especially after the Fuhr! 
Munich speech, ino whleh he admitted Germany \ us 
preparing: fern five-years war—this being a publie 
nvowntl of the failure of that "Blitzkreig” by means of 
which he prottised hts people victory over the demo- 
cracies Inclesa than six months, 


IF AN OFFENSIVE FAILED | 


A few days ayo, at a Counell of War attended by_ the 
Fuhrer, rome Reichwehr chiefs and most of the Nazi, 
Grand Council, General Reichenau spoke on “the capa- 
elty of Germany's resistance In case of failure of an 
offensive in the West," in the course of which the 
Puhrer several times expressed approval by voice and 
gesture, ‘ 


Reichenau remarked that the discussion was taking place { 


in the presence of the Fuhrer himself ond dignitarics 
of the regime and army, and was therefore absolutely 
oMcinl and authorised. Otherwise a discussion of this 
nature would necessarily be consideréd a manifestation 
of “high treason.” 

He declared, in the case of failure of a Western offensive, 
the situation would not be so desperate as certain Reich 
pessimists liked to say. Such failure, moreover, would 

¢ possible only If the Reich decided to launch a’ gen- 
eral attack In the West, which was not the case at 
present. i 

He pointed out that before this would take place indus- 
trial and agricultural reorganisation of the Reich would 
be completed, and the entire industrial system trans. 
ferred from the left bank of the Rhine and exposed 
regions of the right bank to the well-protected hinter- 
Innd—especially the north-east and south-east regions 
of the Relch. : 


) 


He is now inclined to liston to those who urge him to remain on the defensive—for 
But this’ new plan, like others, will fail, ‘ 


Consequently Uf, Ming next spring, the Reich evacunted 
the left hank—tempornrily, of course.—thes, could be 
absolutely certain’ the cotintrs’s wi ccendmy would 
suffer no havin. 


: PEACE—ON CONDITIONS 

Ue went qn to shaw how such evacuation could be car- 
ried out without weakening the country's capaelty for 
rosistance, ‘3 

The German armies could withdraw to the other side of 
‘the Rhine and remain there on the defensive for years. 
Reichenau explained ot length, that such a defe 
would Jiek all danger. This plan had been sturgested 

_ bY a group of German ofiicers to Ludendorgt jn 1918, 

“but the Kaiser had not dared to adapt it because the 
economde position thereby wotlld have been greatly 
weakened, . 

Reichenau continued that if the German armies ever 


didi carry out such aowithdrawal it should not affect |. 


the positlon of the Naal reyime. 

With forces established in this defensive position the 
Naz Government woul necept peace only on condition 
the Third Reich maintained’ the frontiers as existing 
in Autumn 1939, and atso its Nazi regime, tee 

The Fuhrer agreed nlso when Relehennu stated that if 
such a withdrawal was carried out the chances of 
obtaining from the democracies a real “German peace" 
demanded by the Fuhrer would continually incrense. 


BOGEY OF BOLSHEVISM 

For Reichenau harbours the Mlusion that the Allies, fear- 
ing for spread of Bolshevisation and Russinn military 
intervention with this aim, would’ be oblipedt to come to. 
terms with the Fuhrer, and agree to the maintenance 
of n strong German Army as o bulwark against Com- 
munism, . , 

Reports of this discussion deeply impressed Nazi circles, 
for it is the first time the Fuhrer has given. his advisers 
to understand that he will probably not dare to launch 
a general attack on the Western front, but that he 
considers he can, while remaining on the defensive, 
obtain that German peace for which he has for so 
many years asked the Third Reich to make such great 
sacrifices. ; 

One may presume, therefore, that if the Fuhrer treats 
Holland as Stalin has treated Estonia, he will launch 
an aly war agatnst England while trying to effect (a 
moral conquest” .of neutral countries, 

Ile has just sect up an orgonisation, with a fund of 
£5,000,000 for distributing publicity to neutral coun. 


tries. 

Under the direction of Dr. Gemsen, German publicity 
associations have been formed in various neutral 
capitals, and newspapers ore offered large sums of 
money on condition that they modify their policy in 
favour of Germany. oot 

But the Fubrer’s new plon will fail as others have,

PARIS, Nov. 20, 

There fs now no aspect of the work 
being performed by the British 
Army jn the field with which the 
Ministers of the British Dominions 
who are visittast France are not fully 
familar, 

To-enight they have completed two 
strenuous days of visits, talks and 
travel which cnabled them to sec for 
themselves a great part of the British 
front, to learn from the corps, 
divistonal and brigade commanders 
{he plons on which they are basing 
{her prepnrations, and to form 
their own opinion on the work 
which has already becn done by the 
B.B.E. since it took over its sector on 
the Allied front. 

The political representatives of the 
Dominiens are cach aecompunied by 
one senior military adviser, All are 
agreed that the work performed in 
few, short weeks ts prodigious, 

They are Impressed by the spirit 
which antinates the British Army, 
from the Commander-in-Chicf, Cen. 
Viscount Gort, and the corps com 
minders down to the guardsmen and 

soldiers whom’ they have 

ed on digging, revetting, 

developing. the = de-

mig nuints taken over from) the 
Freneh,

“Dally Herald Navat Correspondent 


FROM the raimswept bridge of a mine-sweeper oul at Bed 


IT have just watched = specding 


destroyers: and anti-submariie 


patrols “on the hunt” after the U-boat. 


Overhead, a flying-bout roared. 

An onemy submurne had been 
detected overnight In certain waters 
towards which we were ordered. 

Through the early morning misty 
our long ‘ling of minesweepers went 
out to drag the seas for any minefield 
Ue. U-boat might have laid in the 
darkness, 

“If anything goes wrong,” a ship's 
offleer warned me, “just grab a belt 
and go over the side as fast us you 
can,” 

To the peril from exploding mines 
there was ndded the possibility of 
toroedo attack at any moment. 

But the very able seamen I talked 
to on the lower deck are proud of 
the risks they ore taking, 

Emargency Call 

They told me how until this sum- 
ner they ‘were men of the trawler 
fishing fleet. 

THI then they fshed for food In 
peiceful waters. Now, every day, 
us men of the R.N.R., they seour the 
seas for danger, 

Soon the ship’s radio burred out 1 
tapid series of calls, ; 

Out of the mist,.to starboard, de- 
stroyers streaked mast us at ‘three 
times our speed 

The fying boat zoomed overhead. 
An emergency call had gone ‘out. 
The destroyers were after a U-boat. 

But the men. of the minc-sweeping 
flatitla went stolldly dn with the job 
of senreching for mines. Theirs is 
no job of spectacular speed. - 


In Line Abreast 


The sweepers in line nbreast were 
linked together by wire  hawsers, 
dragged through sca channels at 
varying: depths to catch the mooring 
rope of any possible mindgs., 

The towing strain tears the mince 
from its anchoraze and nds it 
floating, {0 the surface to ‘be destroyed 
by gunfire, 

From ‘the flotilla flagship came ao 
flashed = instruction. We were to 
follow a certain course for solo 
sweeping. 

The sweeps to be used looked Uke 
urey painted torpedocs. 

Once overboard, with flag markers 


‘lin their backs, they: ride the sens 


like glant harpooned fish, 
Adventurers All . 

Below the surface wire ropes 
connect them to heavy “Icites’’ lee 
an outsize in farm harrows ~ 

Mine sweeping Is dangerous. But 
it spells adventure. There. is no lack 
of volunteers. 

The one thing the Germans want 
to know is where our mine-sweeping 
dects.are working—and how they're 
doing, That's why I can no more 
reveal where we have been sweeping 
than I can give details of our “catch.”

MARRIED 


AN interesting wedding was 
held at the Registry yester- 
day when Mr. William George 
Maycock married. Miss Eva 
Helena Laing before Mr. T. S. 
Whyte-Smith, Registrar. 

The bride is the daughter of 
Mr, and Mra. R. K, Laing, and 
sister of the well-known Dr, 
Douglas Laing. She wore a smart 
outfit of white sharkskin. Her 
silver veil was held in place by 
a coronct of orange blossoms.. 
Candytuft composed her bouquet. 

‘The bridesmalds were the Misses 
Constance Sully, Cissy Abbass and 
Gertle Laing. Miss Sully ware ornnge 
taffeta trimmed with brown, and her 
brown veil was caught to her head 
by orange flowers. The other brides- 
maids wore Invender taffeta with 

purple and blue ribbon belts. Laven- 
der flowers held their blue veils in 
place, Each carried a posy of mixcd 
flowers. 

The bridegroom {a the bon of Mr. 
and Mrs, J. H. Maycock, and was 
educated at the Central British 
School. He {gs -now a partner af 
Messrs.-A. Dransfleld & Co., Manu 
facturers' Representatives and Im- 
porters and Exporters, 

Mr. R. K. Liang gave his daughter 
away in marringe, and Mr. J. H. 
Maycock,. Jr., attended his brother 
os best man, 

After the ceremony a reception, 
followed by a dance, was held at the 
Gloucester Hotel. 

When Mrs. Maycock ‘left for the 
honeymoon, which fs being spent at 
Repulse Bay, she wore an ornnge 
coloured dress with brown felt hat 
and accessories, and fur cape {ol 
match, i

that direction? Mr, Eden, with 
some confidence, gave — cssurances 
that such things were on their way, 

Throughout the day the party was 
accompanied by the Duke of Clouces- 
ter, who js now fully familar with 
every Inch of the British front and 
buck areas, 

Lord Gort, however, .left to the 
respective Corps Commanders the 
duties of the day and did not join 
the party until dinner-time.

Dominions visitors were taken to a

proof was sriven to me by no 60-

| Hungarian friend.

Munich Reactions

the day of the attempt upon’ Hitler’s 
life, and told me that there it is 
commonly realised. by all, except the 
fanatleally Nazi group, that this was 
a further examplé of, the Relchstag 


fire technique,

nouncement of the affair now, as 
then, was made far too rapidly after 
the event to be possible in a country 
as strictly censored os Germany un~ 
less special instructions denoting pre- 
vious knowledge of the event had 
been issued.

early thls morning. 

Four men, two of them armed with 
revolvers, forced their way into the 
second floor of 172 
Street, where they held up the oc- 
cupont; Fung Kwal-shing (73) who 
$s described as a. Jandlord, and forced 
him at the point of the revolvers to 
hand over the keys to his safe. 

They then ransacked the safe, 
taking from it $15,500 In Hongkong 
currency, $1,150 In Chinese currency 
and $500 in Singapore notes, 

They also stole a diamond ring. 

Half an hour after thelr entry iato 
the house, the men decamped with 
their booty. 

A police net has now been spread 
over the Colony in a search for the 
robbers, As yet no arrests have 
been made.

Australia 
May Export 
Wer Material 


AUSTRALIA is. industrially 
prepared to mect war conditions, 
und both Government and pri- 
vate factories are being called 
into action to assist the Allies. 

“In 1914 Australla had to depend 
on Great Britatn for munitions,” sald 
Mr, James Hendry, president of the 
N.S.W. Chamber of Manufactures, 
“To-day it is most Hiely that Aus- 
tralla wil} not only help to feed the 
Allled armies, but will export munl- 


tions, 
Mobilised Industry 


. “Now that the call has come, the 
manufacturers of Australia will res- 
pond with all the resources at their 
disposal, Australla’s safety may well 
depend on the ability of Australian 
industry to supply all the many and 
varied requirements of modern me- 
chaonised ivarfare." 

Australia now makes almost every- 
thing necessary for modern warfare 
from acroplanes to motor-cars, states 
the British United Press. «For nearly 
thiee years the Government has becn 
mobilising private Industry, os well 
os such units as Government railway 
workshops to supplement Govern- 
ment munition factories.

By DOUGLAS WILLIAMS.

; WITH THE B.E.F. IN FRANCE 
THE switchboard was housed in a 


cellar. Three 


soldiers, the Corps’ of Signals badge on their tunics, 


worked feverishly without 2 moment’s pause, their voices |M¢n Keine, home on Tenve. 


murmuring an incessant chant as they pushed in or 


[pulled out plugs completing 


calls. 


The multi-panelled board at which they sat was 
2 maze of cords and switches, but they handled it all 
with the nimble fingers of busy hotel operators. 
There was no excitement but quick, efficient co-opera- 


tion. 


Through a half-open door leading into another and |oitte. or Hirings 


larger cellar came the clack of teletype machines as the 


trained fingers of the 
operators typed. out mes- 
sages in flowing columns 
-of paper. : 


I stood in the nerve centre of 
the B.E.F.—in the headquarters 
of Signals. : 


Throurh this centre pass day and 
night oll the myriad communications 
of an army in the fleld, from White- 
hall to Viscount Gort, from the 
Commander-in-Chief_ In_turn to his 
Corps and Divisional Commanders, 
from the Quurterinaster Gencral to 
his supply bases, and lines of com- 
munication from the British Army to 
the French Army, : 


A ceascless flood of telegrams ts 
handled by the Corps Signals with 
the same frictlonless case as though 
they were passing through the post 
office at home. 


Nearly 500 calls 
vitechboard receives, 


an hour the 
incoming and 


tgolng, "more: than~700-teleyrams-a- 


ay piss over the electrical teletype 


machines. 


And this In a quict period of 
preparation and organisation. It 
needs little imagination to think 
to what an extent this traffic 
would suddenly be swollen In the 
event of military activity, some 
sudden move by Hitler or the im- 
minence of actual fighting opcra- 
tions. 


Yet I was assured there would be 
no hitch, no over flooding, that all 
would be taken care of us speedily 
and as efficiently, :

FRANCE, 

ATHER CHRISTMAS has, made 

what may _secm a premature 
appearance in France. 

In the red-tabbed person of the 
Director of Hirings he has turned up 
at n newly-opened teave port and has 
yented enough houses and buildings 
fo make an adequate rest camp for 


It will be ready and comfortable 
before the first butch arrive in the 
middle of next month to embark on 
their leave boat. 

For obvious’ military reasons it is 
not possible to say where this port 
Is, but ft can be said that the men 
wilt nat be wasting a great deal of 
time.on the journey: home, 


Billcts—Better Than Ever 


In spite of his unromantic litle the 
Is the man to 
whom the Army owes thanks for 
billets more comfortable’ than any 
army on active service has ever 


4'enjoyed before. : 


He, his handful of officers nnd 100 
men, are responsible for housing the 
entire expeditionary force, from the 
Commander-in=Chief down to: the 
smallest bugier; and almost without 
exception, the whole lot sleep with 
roofs over their bends. ° 7 

Ordinary billets cost (he Dircctor of 
Hirings three franes a day for oflcers, 
one frame for N.C.O.and 70 centimes 
for men. It is not a Tot when trans-{ - 
Inted Into terms of sterling, but the; 
price is fixed ‘by the French autho- 
rities, and they consider it adequate.

Britain and Free Speech 


A day seldom pasges in which 
some London newspaper docs not 
complain of the burden of war-time 
officinidom restricting the freedom 
of British life. A render of the 
London press, tecently arrived in 
England, might come to the con- 
clusion that Britain had ceased to 
be a democracy and was now 
woverned by 2 dictatorial bureau- 
eracy. . 

Yet if he asked himself how he 
had come to such, a conclusion, he 
vould. surely think _it__significant. 
-thut he had been led to it by read- 
ing the public press. Was there 
ever a purely authoritarian State

LONDON, Dec. 19 (Reuter). 
—The arrival of tho first 
Canadian ‘Expeditionary Force 
has aroused great interest, and 
newspapers give them a grea 
deal of space, : 

The frst news of the Canndians’ 
arrival was given by Mr. Winston 
Churchill, the First Lord of the 
Admiralty, in a brondcast, 

‘Even the people of the port at 
which they landed did not realise who 
they. were untll the men spelt out in 
unison, C-A-N-A-D-A and then 
shouted “CANADA,” 


~~ Route Mareh To Camp 


They were promptly given a warm 
welcome. Those on shore threw 
fhem packets of clgarcttes, the 
Canulians replying with apples. 

- They were transferred’ to the base 
by traln and marched through the 
quiet country headed by their band, 

They found their camp fitted with 
every amenity, including Iron beds 
with mattresses, hot and cold water 
shpwers, o large mess room and an 
up-to-date kitchen, 

They have already named their 
camp “Hore-Bellsha Palace.” °

Ali Baba 
At The 
Y.M.C.A. 


THE opening to-day of 
“Ali Baba”, this year’s 
Y.M.C.A. Christmas enter- 
tainment, marks the golden 
jubilee in Hongkong of the 
presentation of this popular 
“panto” by local amateurs. 

By a remarkable coincidence, 
it was. fifty years ago this 
Otiristmas that the Hongkong 
Amateur Dramatic Club made 
their debut in burlesque when 
they presented “Ali Baba" at 
the Theatre Royal, City Hall. 
The show ran until January 4, 


| 1890. 


The “Telegraph” of December 27, |, 


1889, says: “Ifis Excellency Governor 
a of the Colony 
were present to witne the novel 
tnd somewhat daring, experiment of 
our tocol amuteur talent In an al- 
topether new field. ‘ 

“Ali Baba and the Forty Thieves" 
isan old extravaganzn that) was 
Played in London with considerable 
tuccess many years ago; the version: 
played fast night has, of course, been 
modernised and localised to sult the 
altered character of the times. The 
plot is based on the ancient story In 
the “Agri n Nights’, 

GAGGING" IN 1889 

“The piece itsel€ fs about as 
threadbare as even n modern. extra- 
vaganzn could be, but as it affords 
opportunites for any smount of 
‘gagging’ and so-called wittielsms, 
and for the introduction of musien! 
selections, comtc and otherwise, it 
serves the end for which it was 
written, ', 

“Before proceeding further we 
have to compliment Captain Clayton, 
the stage manager of the A.D.C,, on 
the manner fu which ihe plece was 
staned, the scenery being exceptional- 
ly effective. s 
' “Great credit is certainly duc to 
the conductor, Mr. Hill, the band- 
master of the Argyle and Sutherland 
Highlanders, and to the orchestra 
generally for the accuracy and pre- 
cision with which the musical num- 
bers were dealt with,” 

And now to-day, fifty years ofter 
that first local performance, “All 
Baba" wilh again be presented to the 
Hongkong public. 

A dress rehearsal for to-day's show, 
was hetd on Monday night and was 
attended by oo large number 
Service men. 

The pantomime, whielt will run 
dur.ng the Christmas festivities, Is 
being produced by Major Brown. 
Tne make-up for certain members 
of-the-cast-is-being-done-by~Victor 
Mamak.

Canada’s Ban On 
German Films 


THE ban on trade with the enemy 
under the War Mensures Act in 
Canada offects the acceptance in the 


“Dominion-of-any~motlon™pictures~of 


German origin, whether throu, 
neutral country ‘or not. 


“Through the regulations of the Act 
{t is on offence for any firm or in- 
dividual in Canada to accept im- 
portation of any commodity throurh 
which the enemy country derives 


gh 


any assistance in the way of revenue,| 


whether a neutral third party is ins 
volved or not, 


This forbids the importation of any 
moving picture Alm, Including news- 
reel clip, any revenue from which 
would accrue to the credit of the 
enemy,

KING AT 
PLYMOUTH 


London, Dec. 19. 
Itis Majesty the King paid a sur- 
prise visit lo the Naval Command at 
Plymouth yesterday and spent many 
neues inspecting the naval establish- 
ment, 


He talked to scores of officers, petty 
oMvers’ und men, Including those of 
the mine-sweeping and anti-sub- 
marine trawlers attached to the Fleet. 


The King also took the salute from 
a parade of 2,000 of the 9,000 officers 
and omen ‘stationed at Plymouth. 
They included fishermen from New- 
foundland, for whom the Kinyo had oo 
special word of precting.—Retter

ADAME TABOUIS; WORLD-FAMOUS DIPLOMATIC WRITER, REVEALS TO-DAY THAT 
HITLER, STILL IN A STATE OF INDECISION, HAS AGAIN CHANGED HIS MIND ABOUT 


FUTURE PLANS. ‘ 


Ho hae for the first time givon his advisers to understand that he fears to launch an offensive 


in the West., 
years, if necessary. 


HILE German threats to’ Britain are still 
unexecuted the Reich, in the last few 
weeks, has suffered a succession of setbacks of 
all) kinds—the Anglo-French-Turkish Pact, 
Papal Encyclical, raising of the U.S. arms em- 
bargo, speeches by Molotov and Voroshilov 
affirming once more that the Soviet Union will 
not give the Reich military or material support, 
also that the Kremlin officially numbera Ger- 
many among the capitalist States: it wishes to 


oppose. 


The effect on the Fuhrer of this series of defeats can be. 


fauged by reporta I have received from neutral coun- 
tries, sald Madame Tabouls. 

Tho war plans for: the West we have heard sq much 
‘about have undergone considerable changes, 

Yt seems that the Fuhrer, while adopting towards Holland 
o similar attitude to that adopted by the Soviet to the 
Baltic States, now wishes to prepare his generals for a 
new plan. J 

This Is of much intercst to us, especially after the Fuhrer’s 
Munich speech, in which he admitted Germany was 
preparing for a five-yenrs war—this being a public 
avowal of the fotlure of that “Blltzkreig” by means of 
which he promised his people victory over the demo- 
cractes in less than six’ months. 


IF AN OFFENSIVE FAILED 


A few doys ago, at a Council of War attended by the 
Fuhrer, some Reichwehr chiefs and most of the Nazi 
Grand Council, General Refchennu spoke on “the capa- 
city of Germaony’s resistance in case of failure. of an 
offensive in the West," in the course of which’ the 
Fuhrer several times expressed’approval by voice and 
gesture. 

Reichenau remarked that the discussion.was taking place 
in.the presence of the Fuhrer himself and dignitaries 
of the regime and army, and was therefore absolutely 
oficial and authorised. Otherwise a discussion of this 
nature would necessarily be considered ev ‘manifestation 
of “high treason.” . ‘ 

He declared, in the case of fallute of a Western offensive, 
the situation would not be so desperate as certain Reich 
pessimists liked to sny. Such failure, moreover, would 
be possible only if the Retch decided to Jaunch a fen- 
eral attack in the West, which was not the case at 
present. , ; ‘ 

He pointed out that before this would take place indus- 
trial and agricultural reorganisation of the. Relch would 
be completed, and the entire industrial system trans~ 
ferred from the left bank of the Rhine ‘and exposed 
regions of the right bank to the well-protected hinter- 
land—especially the north-east and south-cast regions 
of the Reich. 


reat ; 


.

He is now inclined to liston to those.who urge him to remain, on the dofensive-—for 
Burt this now plan, lke othars, will fail. : . 


Consequently if, starting next apring, the Reich evacuated 
the left bank—tcemporarily, of coursc—they could be 
absolutely certain the country's war economy would 

* suffer no harm. . 


PEACE—ON CONDITIONS 


He went on to show how such evacuation could be car- 


ried out without weakening the country’s capacity for |. 


-resistance. 

The German armies could withdraw to.the other side of 
the Rhine and remain thero on the defensive for years, 
Relchenau explained. at length that such a defensive 
would lack all danger, This. plan had been suggested 
by a group of German oMcers to Ludendorff In 1918, 
but the Kaiser had not dared to adopt it because the 
economic position thereby would have been greatly 
weakened, 

Reichenau continued that if the German ormics ever, 
did carry out such a. withdrawal it should not affect 
the position of the Nazi regime. 

With forces established in this defensive position the 
Nazi Government would accept pence only on condition 
the Third Reich maintained the frontiers os ‘existing 
in Autumn 1939, and also its Nozl regime. * * 

The Fuhrer agreed also when Reichenau stated that if 
such a withdrawal was carried out the chances of 
obtalning from the democracies a real ‘German peace” 
demanded by the Fuhrer would continually increase, 


BOGEY OF BOLSHEVISM 
For Refchenau harbours the illusion that the Alles, fear- 
ing for spread of Bolshevisation and Russian military 
intervention with this aim, wopld be obliged to come to 
terms with the Fuhrer, and agree to the maintenance 
of a strong German Army as a bulwark against Com- 


m ] 1 

Reports of this discussion deeply .impressed Nazi circles, 
for it is the first tlme the Fuhrer has given his adviscrs 
to understand that he will probably not dare to launch 
a general attack on the Western front, but that he 
eonsiders he con, while remaining -on the defensive, 
obtain that German peace for which he has for so 
many years asked the Third Reich to make such great 
sacrifices,. 

One may presume, therefore, that if the Fuhrer treats 
Holland as Stalin has treated Estonia, he will lounch 
an alr war against England while trying to effect “a 
moral conquest” of neutral countries. 

He hos. just'set up an organisation ‘with oa fund of 
eine, 000 for distributing publicity to neutral coun- 

en. 

Under the direction of Dr, Gemsen, German publicity 
associations. have been formed in various neutral 
capitals, mand newspapers are offered Jarge sums of 
money on condition that they modify their policy in 
favour of Germany. 

But the Fuhrer’s new, plan will fail as others have,

Industrial RESIDENTS BACK Britain 


School 


| Hon. Mr. R. A. C. North 


Pays Visit To Aberdeen 


The remarkable — progress and 
achlevements of the Aberdeen Indus- 
trint School during {he pant twelve 
months drew forth high pralsa from 
lion. Mr, R. A. C. North, Secretary 
for Chinese Affairs, who fs the chair- 
man‘of the board of manarters of the 
school, when he and Mrs, North paid 
a visit on Monday ond inspected _ 
various departments, 


Mr. F. Filppance of the Botanical 
and Forestry Department wos alse 
present and the visitors were met al 
the school by Father Bernardini, the 
Principal, 
Grimshaw, 


Mr. and Mrs, North and the other 
visitors toured the various depart- 


Mients and workshops, and Mra, North 


showed particular Interest in the shop, 
where the boys are busy making toys 
for Hongkong- retailers, The toys 
were rated as high in quality and 
craftmanship as any which before the 
war had been Imported from Japan 
and Europe, 

tc»: 

An Important function was the in- | 
aufturation of the foundry, where the 
first piece of fron was smelted and 
the visltora were able to see the 
Anished work before they left. 


The theatre was ntso visited where’ 
the 200 boys were assembled, They 
Rave the visitora » rousing reception 
and afterwards entertained with a 
{le performance which. was 
highly uppreelated, 

Tho school band under the direc- 
tion of Brother Fantini also gave a 
performanec, 

Two speeches In Chinese and Eng 
lsh were delivered by two of the 
pupils, and before he left, Mr. North 
expressed his thanks for the enter- 
tnining time spent, and his apprecia- 
tion of the wonderful progress. made 
by the school during the past twelve

MR.: LINDSAY LAFFORD 


Former Hongkong Organist 
Now at Princeton 


Mr, Lindsay A, Lafford, former 
organist of St. John’s Cathedral, has 
taken up ‘his new post as Choir 
Director and Organist at Princeton 
University, USA. He made quite 
an impression on hig frat Sunday In 
Chapel. 

Commenting on the event: the 
Princeton Alumnt Weekly says.— 
“Lindsay Lafford, formerly of Here- 
ford Cathedral,. England, who has 
been appointed | to take the place of 
Professor Robert Hufstader os choir 
director and organist, started out his 
first Sundoy. in chapel with a bang, 
Accustomed to:the gentle and melli- 
fluous improvisations of {ts former 
organist, the cholr was startled on 
end when Mr, Lafford sounded forth 
some extra lotict notes 
manner, For o 
few seconds there was no singing 
ond even the drowsy underclassmen 
in the first few pews were aroused 
for a short time.” 

Mr, Lafford arrived in Hongkong 
in April, 1935 and Ieft early in March, 
this year, He was very popular in

of the blockade,

LORD GORT TALKS 
OF HIS TASK 


War Zone Lectures By 


Corps Commanders 


. PARIS, Nov, 20. 

There Is now no aspect of the work 
being performed: by > the British 
Army In the field with which the 
Ministers of the British Dominions 
who are visiting France are not fully 
famillar, 

To-night they have completed two 
strenuous days of visits, tolls and 
travel which cnabled them to sce for 
themselves a great part of the British 
front, to learn from the corps, 
divisional and brigade commanders 
the plans on which they are basing 
their preparations, and ta form 
thelr own opinion “on the worl: 
“which has ulrendy been done by the 
BEF. since It tools over its sector on 
the Allied front. 

The political representatives of the 
Dominions are each accompanied by 
one senfor military adviser. All are 
wgtreed that the work performed int 
few short weeks is prodigious, : 

They are‘impressed by the spirit 
whieh animates the British Army, 
from the Commandcr-in-Chief, Gen. 
Viscount Gort; and the corps com- 
manders down to the guardsmen and 
private soldiers whom they . have 
seen engaged on dinsing, revetting, 
und generally developing the 
fensive system of pillboxes,. forts and 
strong points taken ovér from the 
French, 


Liaison With the French 


Yesterday the Dominions Ministers 
were received by Lord Gort, with 
whom they: lunched before being 
given a full statement by the Com- 
mander-In-Chief on the, nature of 
the task entrusted by Gen, Gamelin 
to the British forces, : 

Their later afternoon was spent in 
n visit to the headquarters of the 
British Army co-operation alreratft. 
where the-commanding officer, with 
the ald of mnps explained-the precise 
functions of the air Units under his 
control. | 

Before dinner there was 2 first 
meeting with the chicf French Army 
Maison -Offlcer with G.H.Q. The 
French forces link up with the 
British at two points, and the Domin- 
jon representatives, straight from 
their inspiring talks with Gen, 
Gumelin ond M, Daladicr, were 
obviously woll satisfied that this 
Nalson Js fully established, - 

On.the morning of their departure 
from Paris the visitors had. been 
roused from thelr beds before 5 a.m. 
by an ofr raid warning, and on 
arrival at G.H.Q. they were saluted 
by a second, , 

Last night the entire party dined 
with Lord Gort and several of his 
chicf staff officers and remained talk~ 
ing until a late hour,

BSHIRLEY MORE GLOBIOUS 


Lunchcon From Paper Bags 

This morning was spent in the 
First Army Corps area after the 
Corps Commander, Lt.-Gen. Sir 
John Dill, in his headquarters, had 
given a brief lecture aided with 
maps. 


A celose inspection was made “of ay 


French pillbox while the divisional 
commander and his staff and brignde 
commander explained the tactical 
basis on which these strong polnts 
and supporting defences have been 
pinnned. 

Anti-tanis obstacles suMeiently for- 
midable to give on impressive sense 
of security were inspected at. close 
quarters when u move was made to 
see guardsmen and sappers, alded by 
mechanical diggers, completing other 
obstacles to the advance of 4 
mechanised army over tat country. 

There followed a visit ta o howitzer 
battery situated in a pleasant Httle 
orchard further concealed by artl- 
ficial camouflage, after which one 
other drive of some length brought 
the cavalcade to the rendezvous with 
the Second Corps Commander, Lt.- 
Gen, Alan F, Brooke. 

Here a plenic meal was enten by 
the roadside. 


Walk In Mud 


Immedintely after, the Second 
Corps Commander conducted his 
visitors to a high point and with the 
ald of maps pointed out the general 
scheme for the defence of his fronts. 

The next divisionnl commander 
wos met at the foot of o famous old 
fort, to whose summit the party 
scrambled for a further Interesting 
exposition of the -part which his 
troops will be expected to perform. - 
* In the next two hours the entire 
party alternately walked! and motor- 
ed considerable distances, slipping 
and sliding through mud- and_ slush 
to Inspect ot close quarters further 
natural ond artificial tani obstacles, 
concrate forts and trench Mnes. 

Warm tributes were pald to the 
work done {in their sector by  battal- 
fon: of Foot Guards, whose trenches 
and breasteworks, constructed under 
conditlons of no little difficulty 
owing to the state: of ground and 
weather, had a thoroughly satisfac- 
tory lool, aed

uttributed to the rejection by

Greater Reich, :

‘Canossa Hospital at 7.30 p.m, yester- 
day from pneumonia, 


- Only 30 years of ne, Mr. Hung, 
who was Manoger of the Chinese 
eeetion of the Hongkony, Canton ond 
Macao Steamship Company, . and 
Manager of the Sun Wo Steamship 
Company, caves a widow, who is a 
:dzughter of Slr Robert Kotewall and

Hung also leaves n mother, two sis-

No Tung, Jnr, and two brothers.

and tiking a BA, degree In 1933, Mr. 
‘Hung rapidjy established himsct{ jn

ost.

Ove of the greatest handicaps there 
fs In golf is the mental handicop. 
1 read somewhere recently of a 
“freak! match that was based on 
this factor, A low  single-figure 
handicop player paired up with a 
poorer stolfer, and before the match 
offered the usual handicap of strokes 
difference, This was refused, and 
the suggesticn put forward by the 
second man‘that he be allowed Wie 
privilege of saying “boo” at any 
{hree times during the round, and 
there would be no strok¢d differenze. 

Thia was agreed to, and the 
“psychologist” won quite comfortably 
—and with two “boo's" In hand!

Lee Shows Considerably 


More Punch Power Than 
' Ceferino 


Ceferino Garcia, world’s middleweight champion, may 
be rated the hardest middleweight puncher in the world 
to-day, but it was Glen Lee, the American title contender, 
who displayed the punching power in training, stdtes the 
Manila Builletin.. Demonstrating the vicious ‘ mauling 
that enabled him to score victories over the leading mid- 


Garcia 


dleweights in the world, including Garcia, Solly Kreiger, 
and Fred Apostoli, Lee smacked around his sparring part- 
ners with merciless abandon, and had three of the four 
boys who worked out with him sprawled out on the can- 


vas several times. 


He first took on Bobby Wills, 
claimant to the welterweight 
championship .of Japan who re- 
cently, returned to Manila and 
floored him three times in the 
first round. He eased up in the 
second round but even at that 
the Japanese champion called it 
quits after the second canto. 


Lee, then turned his guns upon 
Vic Vicente, another ~145-pounder, 
but’ he too quit after being dumped 
in the first round. Johnny Cabuco 
next went in ngainst the Nebraskan 
and lasted one round, ; 

Just to show that he not only has 
a punch but also has plenty of 
speed, Lee next tool on Franeisco 
Faren, .o lightweight, whum he 
knocked down three times in the 
three-minutes of workout.

* Assaults Renewed 

NELSINGFORS, Dec. 19 (Reuter). 
-~Soviet plines yesterday raided the 
outskirts of Helsingfors jn a renewal 
of their aerial assault ‘upon the 
Finnish capital. 

Eighteen muchines took part in the 
raid, but only four managed to get 
through the barrage of anti-alrerntt 
shrapnel sent up from the city's AVA. 
defences. 

The flest’ alarm was -sounded at 
11.20 a.m., the All Clear being given 
at 12,15 pom, 

Shortly afterwards, Soviet planes 
raided Viborg, one being shot down 
by A.A. fire. There were no Finnish 
cnsualtios, : 

The raiders approached Helsingfors 
in scattered formation from the south, 
and the AA. guns immediately went 
into action, two planes being imme- 
diately shot down when lhey tuttempt- 
ed to bomb the airport, 3 a 

Further ralds are expected In view 
of the clear weather now prevailing,

monstration of Empire unity when 
men from the Royal Cnnadian Air 
Foree, the Australian Air Force, the

gether in safe Canadian flying traine 
ing schools.”

Comparative Efficiency 

In connection with Sir Kingsicy 
Wood's estimate, that the Empire 
Training Scheme would put our air 
superiority beyond challenge, In- 
formed air circles here give interest- 
ing facts comparing the relative 
efficiency between the Allied and 


*|German air forees to-day when they 


point out that not one single British 
fighter has been -shot down in com- 
bat since the outbrenk of war, while 
the R.A.F, Command aircraft have 
‘brought down nearly half the German 
bomber reconnaissance nireraft on- 
Baged by them during the first two 
actlve months of the war, 

During 51 raids over British Jand 
or sea in October and November, 21 
German raiders were destroyed. Of 
these, the majority were seen ta 
strike the ground or water, It is 
known that many more failed to re- 
turn to Germany, 

In all these engagements, only one 
British pilot has been wounded, and 
that slightly.

IF AN OFFENSIVE FAILED | 


A few days ufo, at a Council of War attended by the 
Fuhrer, some Reichwehr chicfs and most of the Nazi 
Grand Council, General Reichenau spake on “the capn- 
city of Germany’s resistance in case of failure of an 
offensive in the West,” in the course of which the 
Fuhrer several times expressed approval by volee and 

+ gesture. : 

Refchenau remarked that the discussion was taking place 
in the presence of the Fubrer himself and dignitaries 
of the regime:and army, and wos therefore absolutely 
offleint hnd nuthorised. Otherwise a discussion of this 
nature would necessarily be considered a manifestation; 
of “high treason.” ‘ 

He declared, in the case of failure of a Western offensive, 


the situation would not be so desperate as certain Retch }: 


pessimists Nked to say. Such failure, moreover, would 
be possible only if the Relch decided to Taunch a pens 
eral attack In the West, which was not the case at 
present. ‘ 

He pointed out that before thls would take place indus- 
trial and agricultural reorganisation of the Refch would 
be completed, and the entire industrial system trans- 
ferred from the left bank of the Rhine and exposed 
regions of the right bank to the well-protected hinter- 
land—especially the north-cast and south-cast regians 
of the Reich.

PEACE—ON CONDITIONS 

He went on to show how such evacuation could be car- 
ricd out without weakening the country’s enpacity for 
resistance. ‘ 

The German armies could withdraw to the other side of 
the Rhine and remain there on the defensive for years. 
Relchenau explained at Iength that such a defensive 

. would Incke all danger. ‘This plan had been suggested 
by a group of German officers to Ludendorff in 1018, 
but the Kalser had not dared to adopt !t because the 
economic position thereby would have been preatly 
weakened, : : ‘ 

Relchenau continued that if the German armies ever 
did carry out such a withdrawal It should not affect

With forces established In this defensive position the 
Nazi Government would aceept peace only on condltion 
the Third Relch maintained the frontiers as existing 
in.Autumn 1039, and als Its Nazi regime.

_ BOGEY OF 'BOLSHEVISM

a general attack on the Western front, but that he 
considers he can, while remalning on the defensive, 
obtain that German peace for which he has for so 
many years asked the Third Relcl’ to make such great 
snerifices, 

One mny presume, therefore, that [f the Fuhrer treats 
Holland as Stalln has treated Estonia, he will launch 
an ale war against. England while trying to effect “a 
moral conquest” of neutral countrics. * i

£2,000,000 for distributing publicity to neutral coun- 
tries. *

-associntions have been formed in various neutral 
capitals, and newspapers are offered large sums of 
money on condition that they modify’ their policy in 
favour of Germany. :

‘Graf Spee Sinking 


STOCKHOLM, = Dee. 19 
(Reuter).—The fate of the 
Gyaf Spee is a severe blow 
‘for Germany, says the 
Berlin correspondent of the 
“Dagbladet,” who declares 
that official quarters of the 
capital are very bitter, re- 
garding the refusal of the 
Uruguayan Government to 
allow the battleship an ex- 
tension of time in order to 
enable the necessary repairs 
to be made. 

They accuse Uruguay of a 
breach of international law and 


,| declare that the matter will not 


be forgotten by Germany. 

They allege that Uruguay sub- 
mitted to English pressure. There~ 
fore thelr actlon in sending the Grat 
Spee out of Montevideo “in a state of 
disrepair” was not In conformity with 
strict neutrality, 

Nazis Consolation 

The correspondent adds, however, 
that there is consolation in Germany 
that the Deutschland and the Admiral 
Scheer.can still harass English ship- 
ping, 
be obliged to operate far away fram 
their home bases. ; 

The Berlin representative of the 
“pidningen" says that mourning for 
the Graf Spee is combined with 
gratification that the battleship. did 
not fali into English hands, 

“Cheap Victory” Prevented 

The British had counted on a cheap 
victory, says the German Press, but 
Hitler fooled them by the order to 
blow up the vessel, 
> The newspapers unite in declaring 
that the end of the Graf Speo was an 
honourable one and in conformity 
with the traditions of the German 
Navy.

Is Big Surprise 


LONDON, Dee. 19 (Reuter), 
—The arrival of the — first 
Canadian Expeditionary Force 
has aroused great interest, and 
newspapers give them a great 
denl of space. 

Th first news of the Canadians’ 
arrival was given by Mr. Winston 
Churetv!ll, the First Lord of the 
Admiralty, In a broadcast. 

Even the people of the port at 
which they Janded did not renlise who 
they were until the men spelt out in 
unison, C-A-N-A-D-A and then 
shouted “CANADA.” 

Route March To Camp 

They were promptly given a P iirin 
welcome, Those on shoro threw 
them packets of cigarettes, the 
Canadians replying. with apples. | 

They were transferred to the base 
by train and marched through the 
quict country hended by their band. 

They found thelr camp fitted with 
every amenity, including iron beds 
with mattresses, hot and cold water 
showers, large mess room and an 
up-to-date icltchen, 

They have already named their 
camp “Hore-Belisha Palace,"

LONDON, Dec. 19 (Reuter). 
—More details of yesterday's alr 
battle at Heligoland Indicate the 
probability that British bombers 
shot.down more than 12: Messer- 
schmidts, “Reuter” learns,

_| British aireraft were brought 
own is effectively disproved by 
the fact that the:total number 
of British nircraft engaged ‘was 
nothing like that figure.:

ed for five German fighters. -

tion said: 
““T could see them waiting Uke Mles 
to attack us.” 
25 Messerschmidts Attack 
Between Hellgoland and Wilhelm- 
shaven, 25 Messerachmidts were seen. 
Even when the buttle had begun, 
the British crews continucd on thelr 
course, determined to collect all the 
information possible. 
They also met heavy anti-aircraft 
first from ships and shore, 
Some Germans hung on to the 
* | British bombers when the latter were 
returning, - ; 
One British machine became de- 
tached from the others and scemed 
to be an casy prey. 
Messerschmidts 
force, 
The pilot dived and skimmed the 
sen surface with the enemy on its 
tall, 

Two Messerschmidts venturing too 
élose were immediately shot down 
by the rear gunner, but the ammuni- 
tion was spent. 

A moment later the front gunner 
wns wounded, whereupon the re- 
serve pilot scrambled from the reur 
turret to the front gun, where he 
found plenty of ammunition and 
made full use of St. 

The Brilish alreraft finally shook 
off its pursuers and reached home. 
Claimed Five Nazis

necounted for five Germans,

was forced to descend to the sea 
within 80 miles of the Engllsh coast. 

The following alrcraft prepored to 
drop a rubber boat but saw that the

te considerable booty have been
